has such a factorisation.
We want the
\mathrm{e}(hU\overline{B_{14}}/q)
factor to oscillate suitably to give this cancellation via Lemma
7.2
. The following lemma will ensure that this factor is not degenerate.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
U=a_{0}U_{1}+U_{0}
,
V=a_{0}V_{1}+V_{0}
as in (
5.21
) and in (
5.22
).
If
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
are such that
(a_{0}+a_{1}\nu_{1}+a_{2}\nu_{2}+a_{3}\nu_{3})\in{\mathcal{J}}
, then

(U_{0}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),U_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1.

Proof.

Imagine for a contradiction that
p|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),U_{0}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),U_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. Then
U(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
for all
a_{0}^{\prime}
, and so the equation
UB_{13}+VB_{14}=qq_{3}
(
5.13
) simplifies to give

V(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})B_{14}(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)

for all
a_{0}^{\prime}
. Condition (C5)(d) then implies that
B_{14}(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
does not identically vanish
\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
, so
V_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=V_{0}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
.

By conditions (C1) and (C5)(c),
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
5.16
. But this implies that there is a choice of
a_{0}^{\prime}
such that
B_{14}(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=B_{13}(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
. Evaluating (
5.13
) at
a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
then implies that

q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p^{2}).

This is impossible since
(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
and
q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
is squarefree by conditions (C5)(c) and (C1). This gives the result.
∎

Finally, we need a short lemma to show that we can restrict attention to
q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
being not too small.

Lemma 7.4

(Bounding terms with
q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
small)
.

Let
\tau_{20}>0
and for
\ell=1,2
,
E_{\ell}^{\prime}(X,h;KA)
be the contribution in
E_{\ell}(X,h;KA)
given by the
(\alpha)\in{\mathcal{J}}
such that
|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})|\leq X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-\tau_{20}}
.
Then

E^{\prime}_{\ell}(X,h;KA)\ll\frac{X^{1+\alpha_{0}-\tau_{20}/2}}{N_{P}(KA)}.

Proof.

Since
N_{P}(AK)
is square-free by construction, by Lemma
5.2
, there exists an integer
j
such that
r_{1}\equiv j\ (\mathrm{mod}\ KA)
. The condition
KA|(\alpha)
is therefore equivalent to

a_{0}\equiv{-a_{1}j-a_{2}j^{2}-a_{3}j^{3}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ N_{P}(AK)).

Thus, for any given
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
there are
O(X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4}/N_{P}(KA))
terms
a_{0}
in
E_{\ell}^{\prime}(X,h;KA)
.

We recall that
q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=\prod_{i=0}^{1}L_{i}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
with for
i=0,1
:

L_{i}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=a_{1}+(r_{1+2i}+r_{2+2i})a_{2}+(r_{1+2i}^{2}+r_{1+2i}r_{2+2i}+r_{2+2i}^{2})a_{3}.

If
|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})|\leq X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-\tau_{20}}

then

\min_{i=0,1}|L_{2i}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})|\ll X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4-\tau_{20}/2}.

(7.2)

For any given
a_{2},a_{3}
, the number of
a_{1}
satisfying (
7.2
) is
O(X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4-\tau_{20}/2}).
Since there are
O(X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2})
choices of
a_{2},a_{3}
, the total number of terms in
E^{\prime}(X,h;KA)
is
O(X^{1+\alpha_{0}-\tau_{20}/2})
.
∎

We are now able to bound
S_{1}
suitably.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

First we wish to apply Lemma
7.1
. By (
6.4
), we have
\alpha_{0}<1/20
, so the conditions of the lemma hold if
\eta_{0}
is slightly larger than
\alpha_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
is sufficiently small. This gives

S_{1}\ll(\log H)\sum_{K\in{\mathcal{K}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{A}\\
{N_{P}(A)|{\mathcal{P}}(X^{\theta_{0}})}\\
{N_{P}(A)\leq X^{3\theta_{0}}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{h\leq H^{2}}\frac{|E_{1}(X,h;KA)|+|E_{2}(X,h;KA)|}{h+h^{2}/H}+o(X),

where

E_{\ell}(X,h;KA):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(\alpha)\in{\mathcal{J}}}\\
{KA|(\alpha)}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{e}\Big(\frac{h\ell X}{N_{P}(\alpha)}-\frac{hU\overline{B_{14}}}{q}\Big).

We write
E_{\ell}=E_{\ell}^{\prime}+E_{\ell}
where
E_{\ell}^{\prime}
is the contribution from terms in
E_{\ell}
with
|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})|\leq Y
, and
E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
is the contribution from terms with
|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})|>Y
. By Lemma
7.4
, the contribution to
S_{1}
from
E_{\ell}^{\prime}
is
O(X^{1-\epsilon+o(1)})
provided

Y<X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-4\eta_{0}-\epsilon}.

(7.3)

Therefore we concentrate on the contribution from
E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}
. As in the proof of Lemma
7.4
, there exists an integer
j
such that the condition
KA|(\alpha)
is therefore equivalent to

a_{0}\equiv{-a_{1}j-a_{2}j^{2}-a_{3}j^{3}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ N_{P}(AK)).

(7.4)

Let
\tilde{a}_{0}=\tilde{a}_{0}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3};KA)
be a solution of the congruence (
7.4
). We may write

a_{0}=\tilde{a}_{0}+mN_{P}(KA)
with
m\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})

where

{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}):=\{m:(\tilde{a}_{0}+mN_{P}(KA),a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\}.

(We recall that
{\mathcal{R}}
is the domain defined in (
6.12
).)
This set
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
can be written as a finite union of intervals
I^{\prime}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
.

Any
a_{0}
of the above form ensures that conditions
(C2)
and
(C3)
are satisfied. Conditions (C1), (C4) and (C5) parts (a),(c),(e) don’t depend on
a_{0}
. Thus we find

E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(X,h;KA)\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\ll X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4}\\
q_{2}(a_{1}a_{2},a_{3})>Y\\
\eqref{qij}\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I^{\prime}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
(N_{P}(\alpha),q_{0})=(q,B_{14})=1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{e}\Big(\frac{h\ell X}{N_{P}(\alpha)}-\frac{hU\overline{B_{14}}}{q}\Big)\Bigr|.

Here by
\sum_{\eqref{qij}}
we mean that the summation is constrained by the factorisation condition (
6.11
) .

We now need to control the gcd between
N_{P}(KA)
and
q
. We define
t=(N_{P}(KA),q)
and
t^{\prime}=q/t
. Since
q
is squarefree,
(t,t^{\prime})=1
. We apply Bezout formula (
5.14
)
to separate the congruence in
t
and in
t^{\prime}

and use partial summation to remove the factor

e(h\ell X/N_{P}(\alpha))
.
This gives for
\ell=1,2
, (as in
[
1
, p. 239]
)

E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(X,h;KA)\ll X^{2\eta_{0}+\alpha_{0}/4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})>Y\\
\eqref{qij}\end{subarray}}\max_{B\ll\frac{X^{\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}}}{N_{P}(KA)}}\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m\leq B}\\
{(g(m),t^{\prime})=1}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{e}\Big(\frac{h\bar{t}f(m)\overline{g(m)}}{t^{\prime}}\Big)\Big|,

(7.5)

where
{\mathcal{C}}
is the projection of
{\mathcal{R}}
onto the final 3 coordinates and

f(m):=U(\tilde{a}_{0}+mN_{P}(KA)),\quad g(m):=B_{14}(\tilde{a}_{0}+mN_{P}(KA)).

We recall from (
6.11
) that for all
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
under consideration
q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
factors as
\prod_{i=1}^{7}q_{1i}\prod_{j=1}^{2}q_{2j}
for some integers
q_{ij}
of constrained sizes. We now wish to apply Lemma
7.2
, which requires that for all
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
under consideration and all
p|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
, there is no polynomial
w(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
of degree less than
10
such that
f(X)\equiv{w(X)g(X)}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
.

Let
p|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. By (C5)(e),
a_{3}
is coprime with
p
, and so by (
5.18
),
B_{14}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
is a polynomial of degree exactly two in
a_{0}
since its lead coefficient is
-a_{3}
. By Lemma
7.3
,
(p,U_{0}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),U_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
, and so
U(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
is not identically zero and has degree at most
1
in
a_{0}
. This implies that for all
p|q
, there is no polynomial
w\in\mathbb{Z}[X]
such that

U(X,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\equiv w(X)B_{14}(X,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p)
and we can apply Lemma
7.2
with
k=8
.
We take
r_{0}=q_{22}
,
r_{1}=q_{21}
,
r_{2}=q_{17}
,
r_{3}=q_{16}
, …,
r_{8}=q_{11}
.
By (
6.11
) and (
6.6
), we observe that
q_{17}<q_{1j}
for all
1\leq j\leq 6
.
Let

\theta_{max}+\tau_{max}=\sup_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}(\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}),

(7.6)

where we recall from (
6.14
) that

I_{\mathcal{C}}:=\{(1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(1,4),(1,5),(1,6),(2,1)\}.

Then the sum over
m
in (
7.5
) is bounded by

\begin{split}\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\leq B\\
(g(m),t^{\prime})=1\end{subarray}}&\mathrm{e}\Big(\frac{h\bar{t}f(m)\overline{g(m)}}{t^{\prime}}\Big)\Big|\\
&\ll q^{\varepsilon}B\Big(\Big(\frac{(h,q)(q_{22},t)}{q_{22}}\Big)^{1/2^{9}}+\Big(\frac{q_{22}}{B^{2}}\Big)^{1/2^{9}}+\sup_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\Big(\frac{q_{ij}}{B}\Big)^{1/2^{8}}\Big).\end{split}

We insert this bound into
E_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}(X,h;KA)
, and then subsitute this into
S_{1}
. Writing
q_{22}=X^{\theta_{22}}
, this gives

\begin{split}S_{1}&\ll X^{2\eta_{0}+1+\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}+\varepsilon}\big(X^{-\theta_{22}2^{-9}}+X^{(\theta_{22}+\tau_{22}-\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{2}+6\theta_{0}+10\alpha_{0})2^{-9}}\\
&+X^{(\theta_{max}+\tau_{max}-\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}+3\theta_{0}+5\alpha_{0})2^{-8}}\big)+X^{1-\epsilon+o(1)}.\end{split}

Thus we see that
S_{1}=o(X)
provided

\displaystyle 2\eta_{0}+\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}

\displaystyle<\frac{\theta_{22}}{2^{9}}

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{22}}{2^{9}}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2^{9}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{2}-6\theta_{0}-10\alpha_{0}\Big)-2\eta_{0}-\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{max}+\tau_{max}}{2^{8}}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2^{8}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}-3\theta_{0}-5\alpha_{0}\Big)-2\eta_{0}-\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}.

We recall that
q_{22}=q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})/q_{21}
, that
q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in[Y,X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2}]
and
q_{21}\in[X^{\theta_{21}},X^{\theta_{21}+\tau_{21}}]
. Thus on choosing
Y=X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4-4\eta_{0}-\epsilon}
so (
7.3
) is satisfied, we see that the bound
S_{1}=o(X)
holds provided

\displaystyle 2\eta_{0}+\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}

\displaystyle<\Bigl(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}-\theta_{21}-\tau_{21}-4\eta_{0}\Bigr)\frac{1}{2^{9}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{9}}\Bigl(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}-\theta_{21}\Bigr)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2^{9}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{2}-6\theta_{0}-10\alpha_{0}\Big)-2\eta_{0}-\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{max}+\tau_{max}}{2^{8}}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2^{8}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}-3\theta_{0}-5\alpha_{0}\Big)-2\eta_{0}-\frac{5\alpha_{0}}{4}.

These follow from (
6.3
), (
6.4
) and (
6.8
) on taking
\theta_{0}
sufficiently small and
\eta_{0}
sufficiently close to
\alpha_{0}
.
∎

8
Proof of Proposition
3.2
: The sum
S_{0}

In this section we estimate the sum
S_{0}
from (
3.6
) and establish Proposition
3.2
all under the assumption of Theorem
4.1
.

8.1
The variable
a_{0}
in
S_{0}

With the notation
\alpha=a_{0}+a_{1}r_{1}+a_{2}r_{1}^{2}+a_{3}r_{1}^{3}
, we consider
the subset
{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
is defined by (
6.12
).

For
S_{0}
we proceed in the same way as in
[
1
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
8
]
but with slight differences in some steps where a bound in
O(X^{\varepsilon})
is not always sufficient.

Lemma 8.1

(Removing the variable
a_{0}
)
.

Let
12\theta_{0}+22\alpha_{0}<1
. We have

S_{0}=\Bigl(\frac{4\mathrm{e}^{\gamma}}{3}\log(5/4)\log 2+o(1)\Bigr)\prod_{p<X^{\theta}_{0}}\Big(1-\frac{g(p)}{p}\Big)S_{01}+O(X^{-\alpha_{0}/5}),

where

\displaystyle S_{01}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{G}}}I(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})),

(8.1)

\displaystyle g(p)

\displaystyle:=|\{{\mathfrak{P}}:N_{P}({\mathfrak{P}})=p\}|,

(8.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\exists a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ s.t. }(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\},

(8.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle:=\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}:\,\exists a_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ s.t. }(\alpha)\in{\mathcal{J}}\},

(8.4)

\displaystyle h(q)

\displaystyle:=\mu^{2}(q)\prod_{p|q}\frac{(1-2/p)}{1-g(p)/p}\mathbf{1}_{P^{-}(q)>q_{0}},

(8.5)

\displaystyle I(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})

\displaystyle:=\int_{a_{0}\in{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\frac{{\mathrm{\,d\,}}a_{0}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})},

(8.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})

\displaystyle:=\{a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}:(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\}.

(8.7)

Here
\widetilde{N}_{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
is the quartic form coinciding with
N_{P}(a_{0}+a_{1}r_{1}+a_{2}r_{1}^{2}+a_{3}r_{1}^{3})
on integers.

Proof.

We want to isolate the variable
a_{0}
. We note that the condition
(\alpha)\in{\mathcal{J}}
implies that
(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),B_{14}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
and that
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}
but otherwise there are no further dependencies between
a_{0}
and
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. We use Möbius inversion to detect the condition
(q,B_{14})=1
when evaluated at
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. This give rise to a squarefree
r|(q,B_{14})
which we decompose as
r=r_{1}^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime}

with
r_{1}^{\prime}|N_{P}(KA)
and
(r_{2}^{\prime},N_{P}(KA))=1
. This yields

\begin{split}S_{0}&=\sum_{K\in{\mathcal{K}}}\sum_{A}\lambda_{N_{P}(A)}^{-}\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{G}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{r^{\prime}_{1}|N_{P}(KA)}\\
{r^{\prime}_{1}|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}\mu(r^{\prime}_{1})\\
&\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{r_{2}^{\prime}|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{(r_{2}^{\prime},N_{P}(KA))=1}\end{subarray}}\mu(r_{2}^{\prime})\sum_{\tilde{a}_{0}\in S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{0}\in{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{a_{0}\equiv\tilde{a}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(KA))}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N_{P}(\alpha)},\end{split}

(8.8)

where
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}
are as in (
8.3
) and (
8.4
)

S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime}):=\{0\leq a_{0}\leq r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(KA):r_{1}^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime}|B_{14}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),\,KA|(\alpha)\}.

(8.9)

(We have suppressed the dependence of
S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime})
on
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
for notational convenience.) The inner sum over
a_{0}
is now over points in an interval with a congruence constraint, and so by partial summation (and recalling from (
6.12
) that
N_{P}(\alpha)\gg X^{1+\alpha_{0}/2}
for all
\mathbf{a}\in{\mathcal{R}}
), we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{0}\in{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{a_{0}\equiv\tilde{a}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(KA))}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N_{P}(\alpha)}=\frac{I(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}{r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(AK)}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{X^{1+\alpha_{0}/2}}\Bigr).

(8.10)

The
O(X^{-(1+\alpha_{0}/2)})
error term in (
8.10
) contributes to
S_{0}
a total

\ll\frac{1}{X^{1+\alpha_{0}/2-o(1)}}\sum_{N_{P}(K)\ll X^{5\alpha_{0}}}\sum_{N_{P}(A)\leq X^{3\theta_{0}}}\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}}1\ll X^{-1/4+3\theta_{0}+21\alpha_{0}/4+o(1)}.

(Recall that if
\mathbf{a}\in{\mathcal{R}}
then
\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\infty}\ll X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/4}
by our choice of fundamental domain). This is
O(X^{-\alpha_{0}/4+o(1)})
if
12\theta_{0}+22\alpha_{0}<1
, as in the assumptions of the lemma.

Thus we are left to consider the contribution from the main term of (
8.10
), namely

\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}}\sum_{K\in{\mathcal{K}}}\sum_{A}\lambda_{N_{P}(A)}^{-}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r^{\prime}_{1}r_{2}^{\prime}|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
r^{\prime}_{1}|N_{P}(KA)\\
(r_{2}^{\prime},N_{P}(KA))=1\end{subarray}}\mu(r_{1}^{\prime})\mu(r_{2}^{\prime})\frac{|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime})|I(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}{r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(AK)}.

(8.11)

By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, we have

|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime})|=\prod_{p|r_{2}^{\prime}N_{P}(KA)}|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},p)|,

(8.12)

where

|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},p)|:=\begin{cases}|\{0\leq a_{0}<p:p|(B_{14}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),N_{P}(\alpha))\}|,&\text{if}\ p|r_{1}^{\prime},\\
|\{0\leq a_{0}<p:p|B_{14}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\}|,&\text{if}\ p|r_{2}^{\prime},\\
|\{0\leq a_{0}<p:p|N_{P}(\alpha)\}|,&\text{if}\ p|N_{P}(KA)/r_{1}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

We compute
|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},p)|
using Lemmas
5.16
and

5.17
. Under the condition
P^{-}(q)>q_{0}
we find

|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime},p)|=\begin{cases}2&\text{if}\ p|r_{2}^{\prime},\\
1&\text{if}\ p|N_{P}(KA).\end{cases}

Using this bound in (
8.12
) gives

|S(r_{1}^{\prime},r_{2}^{\prime})|=2^{\omega(r_{2}^{\prime})}.

Inserting this in the previous expression (
8.11
) for the main term of
S_{0}
, we see that the sum over
r_{1}^{\prime}
is
1
if
(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),N_{P}(KA))=1
, and
0
otherwise. Thus the expression (
8.11
) simplifies to

\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}}I(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\Bigl(\sum_{r_{2}^{\prime}|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\frac{\mu(r_{2}^{\prime})2^{\omega(r_{2}^{\prime})}}{r_{2}^{\prime}}\Bigr)h_{1}(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})),

where

h_{1}(q):=\Biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in{\mathcal{K}}\\
(N_{P}(K),q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N_{P}(K)}\Biggr)\Biggl(\sum_{(N_{P}(A),q)=1}\frac{\lambda_{N_{P}(A)}^{-}}{N_{P}(A)}\Biggr).

Recalling that
{\mathcal{K}}
is the set of prime ideals with norm between
X^{4\alpha_{0}}
and
X^{5\alpha_{0}}
, we see that for
q\ll X^{O(1)}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in{\mathcal{K}}\\
(N_{P}(K),q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N_{P}(K)}

\displaystyle=\log(5/4)+o(1),

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(N_{P}(A),q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda^{-}_{N_{P}(A)}}{N_{P}(A)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leq X^{3\theta_{0}}\\
(d,q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda^{-}_{d}g(d)}{d}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{2e^{\gamma}\log{2}}{3}+o(1)\Bigr)\prod_{p<X^{\theta_{0}}}\Bigl(1-\frac{g(p)}{p}\Bigr)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p|q\\
p\leq X^{\theta_{0}}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(1-\frac{g(p)}{p}\Bigr)^{-1}.

Here we used the fact that the linear sieve lower bound function evaluated at 3 is
2e^{\gamma}\log{2}/3
. Putting these expressions together now gives the result.
∎

8.2
Splitting into small boxes

We see from condition (C2) that if
\mathfrak{a}\in{\mathcal{J}}
then
\mathfrak{a}=(a_{0}+a_{1}r_{1}+a_{2}r_{1}^{2}+a_{3}r_{1}^{3})
for some
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
which lies in the region
{\mathcal{R}}
given by (
6.12
). We recall that
\eta_{1}=(\log x)^{-100}
. We cover the region
{\mathcal{R}}
by hyper-rectangles of type

{\mathcal{H}}=]A_{0},A_{0}+\eta_{1}A_{0}]\times]A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]\times]A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\times]A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})].

(8.13)

The number of such hyper-rectangles is
O(\eta_{1}^{-4})(\log X)^{4}=O(\eta_{1}^{-5})
.

Furthermore the contribution to
S_{01}
from hyper-rectangles such that
\min(|A_{i}|)\leq X^{1/4-7\alpha_{0}/8}

is
O(X^{1-\alpha_{0}/8+\varepsilon})
which is sufficiently small.

We will say that
{\mathcal{H}}
is a ‘good’ hyper-rectangle if
{\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}
and

\begin{split}\min(|A_{0}|,|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|A_{3}|)&\geq X^{1/4-7\alpha_{0}/8},\\
\min(|A_{0}|,|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|A_{3}|)&\geq\eta_{1}\max(|A_{0}|,|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|A_{3}|),\\
q_{1}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})&\geq\eta_{1}^{1/10}\max(|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|A_{3}|)^{4}.\end{split}

(8.14)

If
{\mathcal{H}}
is not ‘good’ then we say
{\mathcal{H}}
is ‘bad’. We note that the second and third assertions in this definition corresponds to the
conditions (
4.3
) and (
4.4
).

We denote by
{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathcal{R}}
the set of all good hyper-rectangles. To each hyper-rectangle
{\mathcal{H}}
we associate its projection to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by ignoring
a_{0}
:

{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}=]A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]\times]A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\times]A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})].

(8.15)

Lemma 8.2

(Splitting into small boxes)
.

Let
S_{01}
be as in Lemma
8.1
. We have that

S_{01}\gg\sum_{{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\frac{A_{0}\eta_{1}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}S_{02}({\mathcal{H}}),

where

S_{02}({\mathcal{H}}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
{q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1}\\
{(q,a_{2}a_{3})=1}\\
{(a_{2},a_{3})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)}\\
a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)\end{subarray}}h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})).

We recall from (
6.13
) that
\widetilde{N}_{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
is the quartic form coinciding with
N_{P}(a_{0}+a_{1}r_{1}+a_{2}r_{1}^{2}+a_{3}r_{1}^{3})
on integers.

Proof.

By splitting the sum over
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
and the integral over
a_{0}
into the hyperrectangles
{\mathcal{H}}
, and then restricting only to good hyperrectangles for a lower bound, we find

S_{01}\geq\sum_{{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{{\mathcal{R}}}}S_{01}^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}}),

where

\displaystyle S_{01}^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{G}}}h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))I_{\mathcal{H}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),

\displaystyle I_{\mathcal{H}}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})

\displaystyle:=\int_{A_{0}}^{A_{0}(1+\eta_{1})}\frac{{\mathrm{\,d\,}}a_{0}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}=\frac{A_{0}\eta_{1}(1+o(1))}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}.

We recall from (
6.11
) that if
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{G}}
then
q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
and
q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
factor as
\prod_{i=1}^{6}q_{1i}
and
q_{21}q_{22}
respectively with
q_{21},q_{11},q_{12},q_{13},q_{14},q_{15}
primes satisfying
q_{ij}\geq X^{\theta_{ij}}
. In particular, we see that for any choice of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
there are
O(1)
choices of
q_{ij}
such that
q_{i}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{2})=\prod_{j}q_{ij}
. Thus, summing over these representations, we find

\begin{split}S_{01}({\mathcal{H}})&\gg\frac{A_{0}\eta_{1}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}S_{02}({\mathcal{H}}),\end{split}

say, with
S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})
as given by the lemma and
I_{\mathcal{C}}
defined in (
6.14
). This gives the result.
∎

8.3
Preparation for the application of Theorem
4.1

Following
[
1
, Section 6.2]
or
[
4
, Section 15]
, we do several manipulations in order to take care of the different coprimality conditions and the multiplicative weight
h(q)
. In our situation it is important that we are slightly more careful than these previous works. We do not impose congruence conditions to moduli larger than
(\log{X})^{O(1)}
since this would cause issues related to Siegel zeros (the argument of the previous papers would introduce a congruence constraint of modulus
X^{t_{0}}
for some
t_{0}>0
). This means we need to be careful not to lose the fact that when
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}
, the
a_{i}
are in small intervals.
Let

Z:=(\log X)^{\lambda_{0}},\qquad Z^{\prime}:=X^{\alpha_{0}/10000},

(8.16)

where
\alpha_{0}
is the constant used to define the set
{\mathcal{K}}
(which will be chosen sufficiently small later on) and
\lambda_{0}
is a fixed constant (which will be chosen sufficiently large). From the bound (
8.14
), we certainly note that since
\alpha_{0}<1
we have

Z^{1000}<Z^{\prime 100}<\min(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}).

(8.17)

For brevity we will write

N_{\mathcal{H}}=\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}).

(8.18)

Lemma 8.3

(Removing the condition
(q,q_{3})=1
)
.

Let
S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})
be as in Lemma
8.2
. Then we have

S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})=S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})+O\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z^{3/4}}\Bigr),

where

S_{03}({\mathcal{H}}):=\sum_{d\leq Z}\mu_{(}d)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
d|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
{(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1}\\
{(q,a_{2}a_{3})=1}\\
(a_{2},a_{3})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)\\
a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)\end{subarray}}h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})).

Proof.

First, we detect the condition
(q,q_{3})=1
via Möbius inversion

S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1}\\
{(q,a_{2}a_{3})=1}\\
{(a_{2},a_{3})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)}\\
{a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)}\end{subarray}}h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{d|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}\mu(d).

We split
S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})
into three sums,

S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})=S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})+U_{21}({\mathcal{H}})+U_{22}({\mathcal{H}}),

where
S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})
is the contribution of the terms in
S_{02}({\mathcal{H}})
with
d\leq Z
,
U_{21}({\mathcal{H}})
is the contribution from
Z<d\leq Z^{\prime}
and
U_{22}({\mathcal{H}})
is the contribution from
d>Z^{\prime}
. We note that
S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})
is as given in the lemma, so we are left to bound
U_{21}({\mathcal{H}})
and
U_{22}({\mathcal{H}})
.

First we bound
U_{21}
. Recall that
q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=-a_{1}a_{3}+a_{2}^{2}-c_{3}a_{2}a_{3}-c_{2}a_{3}^{2}
, so the condition
q_{3}\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
implies that

a_{1}\equiv\overline{a_{3}}(a_{2}^{2}-c_{3}a_{2}a_{3}-c_{2}a_{3}^{2})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
. (We restrict ourselves to
(a_{3},q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
so
(a_{3},d)=1
.) Inserting
this into the condition
q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
and multiplying by
a_{3}^{6}
gives

Q(a_{2},a_{3}):=q(a_{2}^{2}-c_{3}a_{2}a_{3}-c_{2}a_{3}^{2},a_{2}a_{3},a_{3}^{2})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
, for a polynomial
Q(a_{2},a_{3})
which is of degree
12
in
a_{2}
(and non-zero). For any given
a_{3}

the number of roots of
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
is
O(12^{\omega(d)})
. For any choice of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
under consideration, there are
O(1)
choices of primes
q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]
with
q_{ij}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. Thus, letting
b(a_{2},a_{3})=\overline{a_{3}}(a_{2}^{2}-c_{3}a_{2}a_{3}-c_{2}a_{3}^{2})
, and noting
Z^{\prime}<A_{i}^{0.99}
(recall (
8.17
)), we deduce

\displaystyle U_{21}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{Z<d\leq Z^{\prime}}\sum_{a_{3}\in[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{2}\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in[A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
a_{1}\equiv b(a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{3}\sum_{Z<d<Z^{\prime}}\frac{12^{\omega(d)}}{d^{2}}\ll A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{3}Z^{-3/4}.

We now consider
U_{22}
. Since
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
, if
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\neq 0
there are
O(X^{\epsilon})
choices of
d
given
a_{2},a_{3}
.
We have
Q(a_{2},a_{3})=0
if and only if
\exists(i,j)
such that

(a_{2}^{2}-c_{3}a_{2}a_{3}-c_{2}a_{3}^{2})+(r_{i}+r_{j})a_{2}a_{3}+a_{3}^{2}(r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}r_{j}+r_{j}^{2})=0,

which rearranges to

a_{2}^{2}+a_{2}a_{3}(r_{i}+r_{j}-c_{3})+a_{3}^{2}(r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}r_{j}+r_{j}^{2}-c_{2})=0.

Since
a_{3}\not=0
,
a_{2}/a_{3}
is a root of
X^{2}+(r_{i}+r_{j}-c_{3})X+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}r_{j}+r_{j}^{2}-c_{2}
and there are at most two such roots. Thus for each choice of
a_{2}
there are at most 2 choices of
a_{3}
such that
Q(a_{2},a_{3})=0
. Moreover, in this case we still have
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\neq 0
, so there are
O(X^{\epsilon})
choices of
d
given
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
. We deduce that (using
Z^{\prime}\ll A_{1},A_{3}
)

\displaystyle U_{22}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{d>Z^{\prime}}\mu^{2}(d)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\neq 0\\
a_{1}\equiv b(a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)\end{subarray}}1+\sum_{d>Z^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})=0\\
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{a_{2}\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{3}\in[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d>Z^{\prime}\\
d|Q(a_{2},a_{3})\\
\mu^{2}(d)=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in[A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
a_{1}\equiv b(a_{2},a_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{a_{1}\ll A_{1},a_{2}\ll A_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<a_{3}\ll A_{3}\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}1

\displaystyle\ll\frac{A_{1}}{Z^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{2}\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
a_{3}\in[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
Q(a_{2},a_{3})\not=0\end{subarray}}\tau(Q(a_{2},a_{3}))+A_{1}A_{2}X^{\varepsilon}\ll\frac{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}X^{\varepsilon}}{Z^{\prime}}.

This gives the result.
∎

Lemma 8.4

(Removing the condition
(q,a_{2}a_{3})=1
)
.

Let
S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})
be as given in Lemma
8.3
. Then we have

S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})=S_{04}({\mathcal{H}})+O\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z^{1/2}}\Bigr),

where

S_{04}({\mathcal{H}}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leq Z\\
s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
\forall\,(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
{[d,s_{2}s_{3}]|q(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
{(s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)}\\
s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)\end{subarray}}h(q(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})).

Proof.

We remove the condition
(a_{2}a_{3},q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
via Mobius inversion, giving

S_{03}({\mathcal{H}})=\sum_{d\leq Z}\mu(d)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{(a_{2},a_{3})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)}\\
{a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)}\\
{d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{s|a_{2}a_{3}}\\
{[d,s]|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}\mu(s)h(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})).

We write
s
as
s=s_{2}s_{3}
with
s_{2}|a_{2}
and
s_{3}|a_{3}
, and write
a_{2}=s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime}
,
a_{3}=s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}
.
Let
U_{3}({\mathcal{H}})
denote the contribution given by the
s>Z
and
S_{04}({\mathcal{H}})

the remaining contribution with
s\leq Z
. Thus we are left to bound
U_{3}({\mathcal{H}})
.

Since each
q_{i}(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})
has a finite number of prime factors in
[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]
, there are
O(1)
choices of the
q_{ij}
, so

U_{3}({\mathcal{H}})\ll\sum_{d\leq Z}\mu^{2}(d)\sum_{Z<s_{2}s_{3}\ll N_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/4}}\mu^{2}(s_{2}s_{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{[d,s]|q(a_{1},a_{2}^{\prime}s_{2},a_{3}^{\prime}s_{3})}\\
{d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2}^{\prime}s_{2},a_{3}^{\prime}s_{3})}\end{subarray}}1.

The form
q
is monic of degree
6
in
a_{1}
(by (
5.24
), (
5.25
)) and
[d,s_{2}s_{3}]
is squarefree, so given
s_{2},s_{3},a_{2}^{\prime},a_{3}^{\prime}
there are
O(6^{\omega([d,s_{2}s_{3}])})
choices of
a_{1}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ [d,s_{2}s_{3}])
such that
q(a_{1},a_{2}^{\prime}s_{2},a_{3}^{\prime}s_{3})=0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ [d,s_{2}s_{3}])
.
Since
(a_{1},a_{2}^{\prime}s_{2},a_{3}^{\prime}s_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}
we obtain

\begin{split}U_{3}({\mathcal{H}})&\ll\sum_{d<Z}\mu^{2}(d)\sum_{Z<s_{2}s_{3}\ll N_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/4}}\mu^{2}(s_{2}s_{3})6^{\omega([s_{2}s_{3},d])}\Big(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{s_{2}}+1\Big)\Big(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{3}}{s_{3}}+1\Big)\Big(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{1}}{[s_{2}s_{3},d]}+1\Big)\\
&\ll ZN_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/4+\varepsilon}+ZN_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/4+\varepsilon}(|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|)+\frac{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{2}}{\min(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}ZX^{\varepsilon}\\
&+\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\sum_{d<Z}\sum_{s>Z}\frac{6^{\omega([d,s])}}{s[s,d]}.\end{split}

This final term is seen to be
O(\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}(\log{Z})^{O(1)}/Z)
. Since
\max(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})\ll N_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/4}
and
Z=(\log{X})^{O(1)}
, this gives

U_{3}({\mathcal{H}})\ll\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z^{1/2}}+N_{\mathcal{H}}^{1/2+\epsilon}.

This gives the result.
∎

Lemma 8.5

(Simplifying the function
h
)
.

Let
S_{04}({\mathcal{H}})
be as in Lemma
8.4
. Then we have

S_{04}({\mathcal{H}})=S_{05}({\mathcal{H}})+O\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z}\Bigr),

where

S_{05}({\mathcal{H}}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\leq Z^{20}\\
d\leq Z\\
s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\ell(u)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
\forall\,(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
{[d,s_{2}s_{3},u]|q(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\\
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
{(s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})=30,\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)}\\
s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}\equiv{30}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)\end{subarray}}1,

and
\ell
is the multiplicative function defined by

\ell(p^{\nu}):=\begin{cases}\frac{g(p)-2}{p-g(p)},&\text{if}\ p>q_{0}\text{ and }\nu=1,\\
-1,&\text{if}\ 7\leq p\leq q_{0}\text{ and }\nu=1,\\
-h(p),&\text{if}\ \nu=2,\\
0,&\text{if}\ \nu\geq 3,\end{cases}

with
q_{0}
given by (
6.1
).

Proof.

Recalling (
8.5
), we see that
h=\mathbf{1}*\ell
where
\ell
is as given by the lemma. In particular,

h(q(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime}))=\sum_{u|q(a_{1},s_{2}a_{2}^{\prime},s_{3}a_{3}^{\prime})}\ell(u).

Since
a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)
and
30|(a_{2},a_{3})
,
(u,30)=1
.
We substitute this into our definition of
S_{04}({\mathcal{H}})
, and consider separately the contribution
S_{05}({\mathcal{H}})
from
u<Z^{20}
and the contribution
U_{4}({\mathcal{H}})
from
u>Z^{20}
.

Since
\ell(u)=0
when there exists
p
such that
p^{3}|u
, we may write
u=v^{2}w
with
\mu^{2}(vw)=1
. Since
U_{4}({\mathcal{H}})
has
u>Z^{20}
, it suffices to separately bound the contribution of terms
U_{41}({\mathcal{H}})
with
w>Z^{10}
and the contribution
U_{42}({\mathcal{H}})
of terms with
v^{2}>Z^{10}\geq w
.

First we bound
U_{41}({\mathcal{H}})
with
w>Z^{10}
. Since
q_{0}>10
, we see that
|\ell(u)|\leq 10^{\omega(vw)}/w
. Following an entirely analogous argument to our bound for
U_{2}({\mathcal{H}})
in Lemma
8.3
, we can find that

\begin{split}U_{41}({\mathcal{H}})&\ll Z\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{2}s_{3}<Z\\
\mu^{2}(s_{2}s_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\geq Z^{10}\\
{wv^{2}|q}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(vw)\frac{(60)^{\omega(vw)}}{w}\Big(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{1}}{wv^{2}}+1\Big)\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}A_{2}A_{3}}{s_{2}s_{3}}\\
&\ll A_{2}A_{3}X^{\varepsilon}+\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}(\log X)Z^{-3}\ll\frac{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z}.\end{split}

Thus we are left to bound
U_{42}({\mathcal{H}})
involving terms with
v\geq Z^{5}
. We see

U_{42}({\mathcal{H}})\leq V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})+\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}V_{ij}({\mathcal{H}}),

where
V_{ij}({\mathcal{H}})
denotes those terms with
q_{ij}|v
for some
q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]
, and
V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})
denotes those terms with
(\prod_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}q_{ij},v)=1
for all
q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]
,

(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}
.

First we consider
V_{21}({\mathcal{H}})
. By (
6.6
), we have
\sum_{i=1}^{6}\theta_{1i}+\theta_{21}>1+\alpha_{0}
. We recall
q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2}a_{3})\ll X^{1+\alpha_{0}}
for all
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}
and that
\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
with
\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}\gg X^{\sum_{j=1}^{6}\theta_{1j}}
. Therefore we must have that
(q_{21},q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))=1
.
Since
\alpha_{0}<1/19
by (
6.4
) and
q_{21}^{2}\leq X^{2\theta_{21}+2\tau_{21}}\leq X^{1/4-7\alpha_{0}/8}\leq\min(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
by (
6.8
) and
(
8.14
), we deduce that

\begin{split}V_{21}({\mathcal{H}})&\ll X^{\varepsilon}\sum_{q_{21}\in[X^{\theta_{21}},X^{\theta_{21}+\tau_{21}}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{q_{21}^{2}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}1\\
&\ll X^{-\theta_{21}+\varepsilon}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}.\end{split}

We now consider
V_{1j}({\mathcal{H}})
. As with
V_{21}({\mathcal{H}})
, we can’t have
q_{1j}^{2}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
by size considerations and (
6.6
). Therefore if
q_{1i}^{2}|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
then
q_{1i}|(q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}))
, and so Lemma
5.13
shows that
P((a_{2}-c_{3}a_{3})\overline{a_{3}})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1i})
. Again, we have that
q_{1j}\leq\min(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
. Thus we have

\begin{split}V_{1i}({\mathcal{H}})&\ll X^{\varepsilon}\sum_{q_{1i}\in[X^{\theta_{1i}},X^{\theta_{1i}+\tau_{1i}}]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{2},a_{3})\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\times[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{P((a_{2}-c_{3}a_{3})\overline{a_{3}})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1i})}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{1}\in[A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{q_{1i}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}1\\
&\ll X^{-\theta_{1i}+\varepsilon}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}.\end{split}

Finally, we are left to bound
V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})
.
Each
v
counted in
V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})
may factored as
v=v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}
, with

v_{1}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{p|v}\\
{p^{2}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}p,\qquad v_{2}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{p|v/v_{1}}\\
{p^{2}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}p,\qquad v_{3}:=\frac{v}{v_{1}v_{2}}.

Since
v
was squarefree, we see that
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}
are pairwise coprime and squarefree.

By Lemma
5.13
again,
P((a_{2}-c_{3}a_{3})\overline{a_{3}})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ v_{3})
. In
V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})
,
v
is coprime with all the
q_{ij}
, and so for any
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle v_{1}^{2}v_{3}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}{\prod_{j=1}^{5}q_{1j}}\ll X^{1+\alpha_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{5}\theta_{1j}}<\eta_{1}A_{2},

(8.19)

\displaystyle v_{2}^{2}v_{3}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}{q_{21}}\ll X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-\theta_{21}}.

(8.20)

Thus we have

V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{s,d<Z}\\
{\mu^{2}(d)\mu^{2}(s)=1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{w<Z^{10}}\frac{10^{\omega(w)}}{w}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}>Z^{5}\\
\mu^{2}(wv_{1}v_{2}v_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{2}\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{a_{3}\in[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{P((a_{2}-c_{3}a_{3})\overline{a_{3}})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ v_{3})}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{1}\in[A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{v_{1}^{2}v_{3}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{v_{2}^{2}v_{3}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}1.

Let
d_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}[a_{2},a_{3}]
denote the discriminant of
q_{1}
(viewing
q_{1}
as a polynomial in
a_{1}
), and
d_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}[a_{2},a_{3}]
denote the discriminant of
q_{2}
. By Lemma
5.13
, we see that the inner sum restricts
a_{1}
to one of
O(6^{\omega(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})})
residue classes modulo
v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}/(v_{1},d_{1}(a_{2},a_{3}))(v_{2},d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3}))
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{a_{1}\in[A_{1},A_{1}(1+\eta_{1})]}\\
{v_{1}^{2}v_{3}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{v_{2}^{2}v_{3}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll 6^{\omega(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})}\Big(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{1}(v_{1},d_{1}(a_{2},a_{3}))(v_{2},d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3}))}{v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}}+1\Bigr).

(8.21)

Let
I_{1}=\{(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4)\}
be the set of the indexes
(i,j)
such that
(r_{i},r_{j})
is involved in the factorisation of
q_{1}
.
We note that

\displaystyle d_{1}(a_{2},a_{3})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i,j),(k,l)\in I_{1}\\
(i,j)\not=(k,l)\end{subarray}}(a_{2}(r_{i}+r_{j}-r_{k}-r_{\ell})+a_{3}(r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}r_{j}+r_{j}^{2}-r_{k}^{2}-r_{\ell}^{2}-r_{k}r_{\ell}))

\displaystyle d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})

\displaystyle=-(a_{2}(r_{1}+r_{2}-r_{3}-r_{4})+a_{3}(r_{1}^{2}+r_{1}r_{2}+r_{2}^{2}-r_{3}^{2}-r_{3}r_{4}-r_{4}^{2}))^{2}.

We remark that the coefficient in
a_{2}^{12}
in
d_{1}
is non zero because we can’t have
r_{i}+r_{j}-r_{k}-r_{l}=0
for two different
(i,j),(k,\ell)\in I_{1}
.
The case
\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}\not=\emptyset
is clear, the other case was noticed in Remark (ii) after the proof of Lemma
5.15
.

For
d_{2}
, it may be the case that
r_{1}+r_{2}-r_{3}-r_{4}=0
. However in this case we can’t also have

r_{1}^{2}+r_{1}r_{2}+r_{2}^{2}-r_{3}-r_{3}r_{4}-r_{4}^{2}=0
since this would imply that
r_{1}+r_{2}=r_{3}+r_{4}
and
r_{1}r_{2}=r_{3}r_{4}
which is not possible when the roots of
P
are distinct. Thus either the coefficient of
a_{2}
in
d_{2}
is non-zero or the coefficient of
a_{3}
is non-zero.

To estimate the sum over
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},a_{2},a_{3}
of the terms with
d_{1}(a_{2},a_{3}),d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})
in
(
8.21
), we write
w_{i}=(v_{i},d_{i}(a_{2},a_{3}))
for
i=1,2
and next forget the coprimality between
v_{i}/w_{i}
and
d_{i}(a_{2},a_{3})/w_{i}
. This sum is thus bounded by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}\geq Z^{5}\\
v_{1}^{2}v_{3}\leq X^{1+\alpha_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\theta_{1j}}\\
v_{2}^{2}v_{3}\leq X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-\theta_{21}}\\
\mu^{2}(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{6^{\omega(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})}}{v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}|v_{1}\\
w_{2}|v_{2}\end{subarray}}w_{1}w_{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{2}\in[A_{2},A_{2}(1+\eta_{1})]\\
a_{3}\in[A_{3},A_{3}(1+\eta_{1})\\
d_{1}(a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ w_{1})\\
d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ w_{2})\\
P((a_{2}-c_{3}a_{3})\overline{a_{3}})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ v_{3})\end{subarray}}1.

If the coefficient in
a_{2}^{2}
in
d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})
is non zero, then the inner sum over
a_{2}
,
a_{3}
is

\ll\eta_{1}A_{3}\Big(1+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{w_{1}w_{2}v_{3}}\Big)12^{\omega(w_{1}w_{2}v_{3})},

otherwise the condition
w_{2}|d_{2}(a_{2},a_{3})
is equivalent to
w_{2}|d_{P}a_{3}
for some
d_{P}\in\mathbb{Z}
depending only of
P
(we recall that
w_{2}
is square free) and thus the inner sum over
a_{2},a_{3}
is bounded by

\ll\Big(1+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{3}}{w_{2}}\Big)\Big(1+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{w_{1}v_{3}}\Big)12^{\omega(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})}\ll 12^{\omega(w_{1}w_{2}v_{3})}\Big(1+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{3}}{w_{2}}+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{w_{1}v_{3}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}A_{2}A_{3}}{w_{1}w_{2}v_{3}}\Big).

Finally we obtain that

\displaystyle V^{\prime}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\ll Z^{2}(\log Z)^{10}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}\geq Z^{5}\\
v_{1}^{2}v_{3}\leq X^{1+\alpha_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{6}\theta_{1j}}\\
v_{2}^{2}v_{3}\leq X^{(1+\alpha_{0})/2-\theta_{21}}\\
\mu^{2}(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})=1\end{subarray}}6^{\omega(v_{1}v_{2}v_{3})}\Big[\eta_{1}^{2}A_{2}A_{3}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}|v_{1}\\
w_{2}|v_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{12^{\omega(w_{1}w_{2}v_{3})}\eta_{1}A_{1}w_{1}w_{2}}{v_{1}^{2}v_{2}^{2}v_{3}^{2}}\Big(\eta_{1}A_{3}+\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{w_{1}v_{3}}+\frac{\eta_{1}^{2}A_{2}A_{3}}{w_{1}w_{2}v_{3}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\ll Z^{-3}(\log Z)^{10}\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}+Z^{3}\frac{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{\min(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}X^{3(1+\alpha_{0})/4-\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\theta_{ij}/2}.

By (
8.14
) and (
6.6
) we see that
X^{3(1+\alpha_{0})/4-\sum_{ij\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\theta_{ij}/2}\leq\min(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})X^{-\epsilon}
. Putting everything together then gives the result.
∎

Lemma 8.6

(Removing
(a_{2},a_{3})/30=1
)
.

Let
S_{05}({\mathcal{H}})
be as given in Lemma
8.5
. Then we have

S_{05}({\mathcal{H}})=S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})+O\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z}\Bigr),

where

S_{06}({\mathcal{H}}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\leq Z^{50}\\
u\leq Z^{20}\\
d\leq Z\\
s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\\
(t,30)=1\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\ell(u)\mu(t)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
\forall\,(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{[d,s_{2}s_{3},u]|q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
d|q_{3}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\\
[t,s_{2}]|a_{2}\\
[t,s_{3}]|a_{3}\\
a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)\\
a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)\end{subarray}}1.

Proof.

Since we have
a_{2},a_{3}\equiv{30}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900)
, we can detect
(a_{2},a_{3})|30
using Möbius inversion
\mathbf{1}_{(a_{2},a_{3})|30}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t|(a_{2},a_{3})\\
{(t,30)=1}\end{subarray}}\mu(t)
and separately consider the contribution
S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})
from terms with
t\leq Z^{50}
and the contribution
U_{5}({\mathcal{H}})
from terms with
t>Z^{50}
. Since there are
O(1)
choices of the
q_{ij}
given a choice of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
, we see that

\displaystyle U_{5}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{t>Z^{50}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\leq Z^{20}\\
d,s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\\
t|(a_{2},a_{3})\end{subarray}}O(1)

\displaystyle\ll Z^{30}\sum_{Z^{50}<t<\min(A_{2},A_{3})}\eta_{1}A_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{2}}{t}+1\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\eta_{1}A_{3}}{t}+1\Bigr)\ll\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{Z}.

This gives the result.
∎

8.4
Application of Theorem
4.1

Lemma 8.7

(Application of Theorem
4.1
)
.

Let
S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})
be as in Lemma
8.6
. Then we have

S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})\gg\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}.

Proof of Lemma
8.7
assuming Theorem
4.1
.

Recalling the definition of
S_{06}
from Lemma
8.6
, we remark that the different conditions modulo
30
on
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
imply that
(q(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}),30)=1
and thus we may impose that
(ds_{2}s_{3}tu,30)=1
.
Splitting
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
into residue classes
\ (\mathrm{mod}\ [t,u,d,s_{2},s_{3}])
, we see that

\displaystyle S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\leq Z^{50}\\
u\leq Z^{20}\\
d\leq Z\\
s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\\
(ds_{2}s_{3}tu,30)=1\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\ell(u)\mu(t)\sum_{{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u)}S_{07}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},[d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u]),

(8.22)

where

\displaystyle S_{07}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},m)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q_{ij}\in[X^{\theta_{ij}},X^{\theta_{ij}+\tau_{ij}}]}\\
\forall\,(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}\\
q_{21}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ D_{q_{2}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\\
{\prod_{j=1}^{6}q_{1j}|q_{1}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{q_{21}|q_{2}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}\\
{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m)}\\
a_{2},a_{3}\equiv 30\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 900),\ a_{1}\equiv 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 30)\end{subarray}}1,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u)

\displaystyle:=\Bigl\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ [d,s_{2}s_{3},t,u]):\,[d,s_{2}s_{3},u]|q(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}),

\displaystyle\qquad d|q_{3}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}),\,[s_{2},t]|u_{2},\,[s_{3},t]|u_{3}\Bigr\}.

We now apply Theorem
4.1
on incomplete norms with
K=\mathbb{Q}(r_{1}+r_{3})
,
\nu_{1}=1
,
\nu_{2}=r_{1}+r_{3}
,
\nu_{3}=r_{1}^{2}+r_{3}^{2}+r_{1}r_{3}
and
\nu_{4}
such that

\nu_{4}
is in the ring of integers of
K
and
(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
K
.
By Theorem
4.1
(taking
X_{i}=A_{i}
,
\ell=5
,
\ell^{\prime}=3
,
\theta_{i}=\theta_{1i}\frac{\log{X}}{\log{A_{1}}}
,
\theta_{i}^{\prime}=(\theta_{1i}+\tau_{1i})\frac{\log{X}}{\log{A_{1}}}
,
\tau=\theta_{21}\frac{\log X}{\log A_{1}}
,
\tau^{\prime}=(\theta_{21}+\tau_{21})\frac{\log X}{\log A_{1}}
), we have that

S_{07}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},m)=(1+o(1))\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{30^{5}m^{3}\varphi(D_{q_{2}})}\prod_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\log\Bigl(1+\tau_{ij}/\theta_{ij}\Bigr).

Here we have used the fact that (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) hold by (
8.14
). Similarly (
4.6
) holds by (
6.3
), (
4.7
) holds by (
6.2
), (
4.8
) holds by (
6.5
), (
4.9
) holds by (
6.6
), (
4.10
) holds by (
6.7
) and (
4.11
) holds by (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) and by noticing that

\frac{4}{1+\alpha_{0}}\leq\frac{\log X}{\log A_{1}}\leq\frac{4}{1+\alpha_{0}/2}
. Substituting this into our expression (
8.22
) for
S_{06}
, we find that

S_{06}({\mathcal{H}})=(1+o(1))\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}}{30^{5}}\frac{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}}{\varphi(D_{q_{2}})}\prod_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\log\Bigl(1+\tau_{ij}/\theta_{ij}\Bigr)\sum_{m\leq Z^{72}}\frac{L(m)}{m^{3}},

(8.23)

where

L(m):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leq Z\\
s_{2}s_{3}\leq Z\\
u\leq Z^{20}\\
(ds_{2}s_{3}tu,30)=1\\
t<Z^{50}\\
[d,s_{2}s_{3},t,u]=m\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\mu(t)\ell(u)|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u)|.

We wish to remove the upper bound constraints on
d,s_{2},s_{3},u,t,m
so we can understand
\sum_{m}L(m)/m^{3}
via an Euler product. Let

\displaystyle L^{*}(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[d,s,t,u]=m\\
(ds_{2}s_{3}tu,30)=1\end{subarray}}|\mu(d)\mu(s)\mu(t)\ell(u)|\sum_{s_{2}s_{3}=s}|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u)|,

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(m)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[d,s,t,u]=m\\
(ds_{2}s_{3}tu,30)=1\end{subarray}}\mu(d)\mu(s)\mu(t)\ell(u)\sum_{s_{2}s_{3}=s}|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u)|,

which are multiplicative functions of
m
. We note that
L^{*}(m)\geq\max(|L(m)|,|\tilde{L}(m)|)
for all
m
and that
\tilde{L}(m)=L(m)
for
m\leq Z
. From the support of
\mu,\ell
we have
L^{*}(p^{k})=0
for
k\geq 3
. We easily check that
L^{*}(p)\leq 2^{5}p
and
L^{*}(p^{2})\leq 3p^{2}
for
p>q_{0}
since
|\ell(p)|\leq 2/(p-2)
in this range. We deduce that
L^{*}(m)/m^{3}\ll\tau(m)^{5}/m^{2}
.
We note that
\tilde{L}(p^{k})=0
for
k\geq 2
and
2\leq p\leq q_{0}
, and
that
\tilde{L}(p^{k})=0
for any
k\geq 1
when
p=2,3,5
.
We find

\displaystyle\sum_{m\leq Z^{72}}\frac{L(m)}{m^{3}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\leq Z}\frac{\tilde{L}(m)}{m^{3}}+O\Bigl(\sum_{m>Z}\frac{L^{*}(m)}{m^{3}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\frac{\tilde{L}(m)}{m^{3}}+O\Bigl(\sum_{m>Z}\frac{\tau(m))}{m^{2}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\prod_{7\leq p\leq q_{0}}\Big(1+\frac{\tilde{L}(p)}{p^{3}}\Big)\prod_{p>q_{0}}\Bigl(1+\frac{\tilde{L}(p)}{p^{3}}\Bigr)+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{Z^{1/2}}\Bigr).

From our bounds on
L^{*}
we see that
\prod_{p>q_{0}}(1+\tilde{L}(p)/p^{3})\gg 1
and the product over
p\leq q_{0}
converges. We wish to show that the product converges to a strictly positive constant, and so need to check that
1+\tilde{L}(p)/p^{3}
doesn’t vanish for some small prime
p
with
7\leq p\leq q_{0}
.
If
p|[d,s_{2},s_{3}]
then for
u=1
or
p
, we have

|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},u,1)|=|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{2},s_{3},u,p)|.

Since
\ell(p)+\ell(1)=0
when
7\leq p\leq q_{0}
,
we deduce

\sum_{[d,s_{2},s_{3}]=p}\sum_{[d,s_{2},s_{3},t,u]=p}\mu(d)\mu(s_{2}s_{3})\mu(t)\ell(u)|{\mathcal{S}}(d,s_{1},s_{2},t,u)|=0.

The value
\tilde{L}(p)
is then

\tilde{L}(p)=1-p-|\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p):p|q(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})\}|.

Then

1+\tilde{L}(p)/p^{3}\geq(p^{3}-6p^{2}-p+1)/p^{3}>0,

when
p\geq 7
.
Thus
\sum_{m\leq Z^{72}}L(m)/m^{3}\gg 1
, and so substituting this into (
8.23
) and using the fact
\tau_{ij}/\theta_{ij}\gg 1
we obtain the result.
∎

8.5
Proof of Proposition
3.2

Proof of Proposition
3.2
assuming Theorem
4.1
.

By Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
,
8.5
,
8.6
and
8.7
in turn, we see that

S_{0}\gg\frac{1}{\log{X}}\sum_{{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{Z^{1/2}}\Bigr).

(Note that in this application Lemma
8.7
we are assuming Theorem
4.1
, and that we have
12\theta_{0}+22\alpha_{0}<1
required for Lemma
8.1
since we are taking
\theta_{0}
sufficiently small and assuming that
\alpha_{0}
satisfies (
).) We note that

\sum_{{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}=\sum_{{\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}\\
{\mathcal{H}}\text{ bad}\end{subarray}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}.

If
{\mathcal{H}}
is bad, then
\max(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})^{4}\eta_{1}^{1/10}\geq q_{1}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
or there exists
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}

such that
|A_{i}|<\eta_{1}\max(|A_{0}|,|A_{1}|,|A_{2}|,|A_{3}|)
. The first inequality implies that there exists
(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}

such that

L_{i,j}(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}):=|A_{1}+(r_{i}+r_{j})A_{2}+(r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}r_{j}+r_{j}^{2})A_{3}|\ll\eta_{1}^{1/40}\max(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}).

Thus, by partial summation

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}\\
{\mathcal{H}}\text{ bad}\end{subarray}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{(i,j)\in I_{\mathcal{C}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=2^{\ell}\\
X^{1-\frac{7\alpha_{0}}{8}}\ll A\ll X^{\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\\
L_{i,j}(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\leq\eta_{1}^{1/40}A\\
\max(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\ll A\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{A^{4}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=2^{\ell}\\
X^{1-\frac{7\alpha_{0}}{8}}\ll A\ll X^{\frac{1+\alpha_{0}}{4}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\\
a_{i}\leq\eta_{1}A\\
\max(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\ll A\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{A^{4}}

\displaystyle\ll\eta_{1}^{1/40}\log{X}.

Similarly, we find by partial summation

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{H}}\subset{\mathcal{R}}\end{subarray}}\frac{A_{0}A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}\eta_{1}^{4}}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})}

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{R}}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\widetilde{N}_{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}

\displaystyle\gg\log{X}.

Putting everything together now gives Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Thus we are left to establish Theorem
4.1
.

9
Incomplete norm forms

In this section we perform our initial reductions to reduce the proof of Theorem
4.1
to that of establishing Proposition
9.13
and Proposition
9.14
. We roughly follow the argument of
[
14
]
in this section, but require a number of small technical modifications.

Let
K
be a quartic number field,
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
its integer ring,
Cl_{K}
its class group. Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
such that
{\mathbf{v}}=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4})
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
K
. We suppose for convenience that
\nu_{1}=1
and
K=\mathbb{Q}(\nu_{2})
. We then define
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{v}}=\mathbb{Z}[\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}]
the order generated by
{\mathbf{v}}
.

We let
N(\cdot)=N_{K}(\cdot)
be the norm on
K
, and note that this is a different norm to
N_{P}
on
\mathbb{Q}(r_{1})
encountered earlier.

There exists an integral basis of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
,
{\mathbf{w}}=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4})
and some integers

w_{ij}
,
1\leq i\leq j\leq 4
, such that

\nu_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}w_{ij}\omega_{i}\quad(j=1,2,3,4).

(9.1)

(cf. for example
[
16
, Proposition 2.11]
).

9.1
From
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{v}}
and vice-versa

We denote by
L_{{\mathbf{w}}{\mathbf{v}}}=(w_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 4}
the matrix of
{\mathbf{v}}
in
{\mathbf{w}}
so that
for all
1\leq j\leq 4
,

\nu_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}w_{ij}\omega_{i}
.

By (
9.1
) this matrix is upper triangular and the absolute value of its determinant is

W=|w_{11}w_{22}w_{33}w_{44}|\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}

(9.2)

Lemma 9.1
.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, there exist
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
with

\alpha=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\nu_{i}

Conversely, there exists a subset
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}\subset\{0,\ldots,W-1\}^{4}
such that for all
{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
we have

\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\nu_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\Leftrightarrow\exists{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{0}:{\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ W).

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
. There exist
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}

and
(a^{\prime}_{1},a_{2}^{\prime},a_{3}^{\prime},a_{4}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Q}^{4}

such that
\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\omega_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}a^{\prime}_{i}\nu_{i}
.
With our previous notation,

\begin{pmatrix}a^{\prime}_{1}\\
a^{\prime}_{2}\\
a^{\prime}_{3}\\
a^{\prime}_{4}\\
\end{pmatrix}=(L_{{\mathbf{w}}{\mathbf{v}}})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
a_{2}\\
a_{3}\\
a_{4}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The matrix
(L_{{\mathbf{w}}{\mathbf{v}}})^{-1}
is of type
\frac{1}{W}(w^{\prime}_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 4}

where the coefficients
w^{\prime}_{ij}
are integers.
This implies the first part of the lemma.

The second part of the lemma is also a direct consequence of the change of basis formula.
With our previous notation we have

\sum_{j=1}^{4}a_{j}\nu_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{4}w_{ij}a_{j}\Big)\omega_{i}.

Then for any
{\mathbf{a}}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
,
\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\nu_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
if and only if
for all
1\leq i\leq 4
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{4}w_{ij}a_{j}\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ W).

The set
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
is the the subset of
\{0,\ldots,W-1\}^{4}
formed by all the solutions of these congruences.
∎

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{a}}
be a principal ideal. Then there is a generator
\alpha
of
{\mathfrak{a}}
such that

|\alpha^{\sigma}|\ll N({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}

for all embeddings
\sigma:K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore there exists
V>0
depending only on
{\mathbf{v}}
such that

\alpha=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}\nu_{i}

for some integers
a_{i}\ll N({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}
.

Proof.

The first part is a particular case of
[
14
, Lemma 4.3]
.
The last part follows also from this lemma combined with Lemma
9.1
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be an hypercube of side length
\delta_{0}B
which contains a point
\mathbf{b}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
such that
\|\mathbf{b}_{0}\|\ll B
.
We suppose that
{\mathfrak{b}}_{0}=(W^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\mathbf{b}_{0})_{i}\nu_{i})
is an integral ideal whose norm satisfies

N({\mathfrak{b}}_{0})=B_{0}^{4}\gg B^{4}
. Let
q
such that
W|q
and
10qW\leq\delta_{0}B
.

Then there exists a set
{\mathcal{W}}({\mathbf{b}}_{0})
of
W^{4}
elements
\beta_{0}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}

with
\beta_{0}^{\prime}=W^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\prime})_{i}\nu_{i}
and with
\mathbf{b}_{0}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
such that for all
\mathbf{b}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

\mathbf{b}\equiv\mathbf{b}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
if and only if
\beta=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{i=1}^{4}b_{i}\nu_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and there exists
\beta_{0}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}({\mathbf{b}}_{0})
with

\beta\equiv\beta_{0}^{\prime}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

Proof.

This is variant of an argument used in the proof of
[
14
, Lemma 9.4]
.

Let
\beta_{0}:=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}({\mathbf{b}}_{0})_{i}\nu_{i}
.
For all
{\mathbf{v}}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{4})\in\{0,\ldots,W-1\}^{4}
, there exists
{\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{u}}({\mathbf{b}}_{0},{\mathbf{v}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}

such that
{\mathbf{b}}_{0}+q({\mathbf{v}}+W{\mathbf{u}})\in{\mathcal{C}}
since
qW\leq\delta_{0}B
, the side length of
{\mathcal{C}}
. We will prove that the set

{\mathcal{W}}:=\Big\{\beta_{0}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}b_{i}^{\prime}\nu_{i}\ \text{with}\ {\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{b}}_{0}+q({\mathbf{v}}+W{\mathbf{u}}({\mathbf{b}}_{0},{\mathbf{v}})),{\mathbf{v}}\in\{0,\ldots,W-1\}^{4}\Big\}

satisfies the conclusion of the lemma.

First we suppose that
\mathbf{b}\equiv\mathbf{b}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
. This implies that there exist four integers
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4}

such that
b_{i}=({\mathbf{b}}_{0})_{i}+qm_{i}
.
We get

\beta:=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\nu_{i}=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}((\mathbf{b}_{0})_{i}+m_{i}q)\nu_{i}=\beta_{0}+\frac{q}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}m_{i}\nu_{i}.

Since
W|q
, this implies that
\beta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
If we choose
\beta_{0}^{\prime}=\beta_{0}+\frac{q}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}v_{i}\nu_{i}+

with
0\leq v_{1},\ldots,v_{4}<W
such that
v_{i}\equiv m_{i}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ W)
then we would have

\beta=\beta_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{q}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}(m_{i}-v_{i}+Wu_{i})\nu_{i}
, and thus
\beta\equiv\beta_{0}^{\prime}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

Now we prove the reciprocal assertion. We suppose that there exists
\beta^{\prime}_{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\beta\equiv\beta^{\prime}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
. Then

\beta=\beta_{0}^{\prime}+q\gamma
for some
\gamma\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
. There exists

g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\gamma=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}g_{i}\nu_{i}
.
For each
i=1,2,3,4
, we have

\frac{b_{i}}{W}=\frac{({\mathbf{b}}_{0})_{i}+q(v_{i}+Wu_{i}+g_{i})}{W}.
This implies that
\mathbf{b}\equiv\mathbf{b}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.
∎

For any ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, we define the function
\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}
by

\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{d}}):=\frac{|\{{\mathbf{a}}\in[1,N({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}]:{\mathfrak{d}}|(a_{1}\nu_{1}+a_{2}\nu_{2}+a_{3}\nu_{3})\}|}{N({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}}.

(9.3)

This function satisfies the following properties.

Lemma 9.4
.

1.

For all degree one prime ideals
{\mathfrak{p}}
with
(N({\mathfrak{p}}),W)=1
, we have

\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{p}})=1
.

2.

We have

\Big|\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in[1,p^{2}]^{3}:p^{2}|N\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\nu_{i}\Big)\Big\}\Big|\ll p^{4}.

3.

For any ideal
{\mathfrak{e}}
such that
N({\mathfrak{e}})
is a power of
p
, we have

\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{e}})}{N({\mathfrak{e}})}\ll\frac{1}{p^{2}}

unless
{\mathfrak{e}}
is a degree
1
prime ideal above
p
.

4.

For any ideals
{\mathfrak{a}},{\mathfrak{b}}
,
\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})=\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{a}})\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{b}})
if
(N({\mathfrak{a}}),N({\mathfrak{b}}))=1
.

5.

For
k\geq 3
, we have

\Big|\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in[1,p^{k}]^{3}:p^{k}|N\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\nu_{i}\Big)\Big\}\Big|\ll kp^{11k/4}.

Proof.

The first four assertions are essentially given by
[
14
, Lemma 7.7]
, except that they work with a basis
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}
in place of
1,\theta,\theta^{2},\theta^{3}
which has a negligible effect on the proof. Indeed, by (
9.1
) the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space spanned by
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}
is the same
as the one spanned by
\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3}
, and the change-of-basis matrix between the basis
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}
and
\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4}
has determinant
W
. Thus when
(N({\mathfrak{d}}),W)=1
we have

\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{d}})=\frac{|\{{\mathbf{a}}\in[1,N({\mathfrak{d}})^{3}]:{\mathfrak{d}}|(a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2}+a_{3}\omega_{3})\}|}{N({\mathfrak{d}})^{2}},

and so it is sufficient to prove these four statements with the basis
{\mathbf{w}}
in place of
{\mathbf{v}}
. The proof is then the same as in
[
14
]
.

We are left to establish assertion 5. Since
N(\nu_{1})\neq 0
, for any choice of
x_{2},x_{3}
,
g_{x_{2},x_{3}}(x_{1}):=N(x_{1}\nu_{1}+x_{2}\nu_{2}+x_{3}\nu_{3})
is a non-zero polynomial of degree 4 in
x_{1}
. Thus, given
x_{2},x_{3}
, if
N(x_{1}\nu_{1}+x_{2}\nu_{2}+x_{3}\nu_{3})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ p^{k})
, we see that
\|x_{1}-\alpha\|_{p}\ll p^{-k/4}
for one of the 4 roots
\alpha
of
g_{x_{2},x_{3}}
over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Thus there are
O(p^{3k/4})
choices of
x_{1}\in[1,p^{k}]
for each choice of
x_{2},x_{3}
. This gives the result.
∎

Let
\gamma_{K}
be the residue in
s=1
of
\zeta_{K}
and we define
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
to be the Euler product
4
4

4

This definition of
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
is slightly different as the one given in
[
14
]
. In the present paper
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
doesn’t depend on some modulus
q^{*}
or
m
.

\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}:=\prod_{\mathfrak{P}}\Big(1-\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{P}})}{N({\mathfrak{P}})}\Big)\Big(1-\frac{1}{N({\mathfrak{P}})}\Big)^{-1}.

(9.4)

Lemma 9.5
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that
for any ideal
{\mathfrak{I}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
R\geq 2
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{N({\mathfrak{d}})<R}\\
{({\mathfrak{d}},{\mathfrak{I}})=1}\\
{(N({\mathfrak{d}}),m)=1}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu({\mathfrak{d}})\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{d}})}{N({\mathfrak{d}})}\log\frac{R}{N({\mathfrak{d}})}=\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}}{\gamma_{K}}\prod_{{\mathfrak{P}}|(m){\mathfrak{I}}}\Big(1-\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{P}})}{N({\mathfrak{P}})}\Big)^{-1}+O\Big(2^{4\omega({\mathfrak{I}})}\exp(-c\sqrt{\log R})\Big).

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as in
[
14
, Lemma 8.5]
.
[
14
, Lemma 8.5]
states the result with
N(J)^{o(1)}
in place of
2^{4\omega(J)}
, but following the proof we see that the error term can be taken as
\exp(-c\sqrt{\log R})\prod_{{\mathfrak{P}}|J}(1-\frac{1}{N({\mathfrak{P}})^{3/4}})^{-1}
, which is clearly sufficient for our slightly stronger bound.
∎

Lemma 9.6
.

For any
2\leq R\leq x
we have

\sum_{N({\mathfrak{d}})\leq R}\mu^{2}({\mathfrak{d}})\sum_{N({\mathfrak{I}})\leq x}\frac{\rho_{v}({\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{I}})}{N({\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{I}})}\ll(\log x)^{8}.

Proof.

By Rankin’s trick, we have

\sum_{N({\mathfrak{d}})\leq R}\mu^{2}({\mathfrak{d}})\sum_{N({\mathfrak{I}})\leq x}\frac{\rho_{v}({\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{I}})}{N({\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{I}})}\leq\prod_{N({\mathfrak{P}})\leq x}\Bigl(1+2\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{P}}^{k})}{N({\mathfrak{P}}^{k})}\Bigr).

By Lemma
9.4
, if
{\mathfrak{P}}
is a degree 1 prime ideal above
p
then the term in parentheses is
1+2/p+O(1/p^{2})
, and if
{\mathfrak{P}}
is of degree more than 1 above
p
then this is
1+O(1/p^{2})
. The result now follows from the Prime Ideal Theorem.
∎

9.2
Multiplication in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{v}}

Definition
.

For any vectors
{\mathbf{d}},{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
, we define
{\mathbf{d}}\diamond{\mathbf{e}}
as be the vector

{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{4}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{4}b_{i}\nu_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}d_{i}\nu_{i}\times\sum_{i=1}^{4}e_{i}\nu_{i}

For
1\leq i\leq 4
we denote by
({\mathbf{d}}\diamond{\mathbf{e}})_{i}
the coordinate
b_{i}
.

This operation is helpful to detect the elements of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{v}}
with a fourth coordinate equal to zero.
The following lemma turns the problem of detecting this zero coordinate into a question about lattices.

Lemma 9.7
.

For any
{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}

let
\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}
be the subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
defined by

\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}=\{{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}:({\mathbf{d}}\diamond{\mathbf{e}})_{4}=0\}.

Then
\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}
is a lattice of rank
3
and
{\mathrm{\,det\,}}(\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}})\asymp\|{\mathbf{d}}\|/D
,where
D
is the GCD of the components of
{\mathbf{d}}
.

Proof.

The argument is essentially a special case of
[
14
, Lemma 7.2]
.
We will expose it in a more pedestrian way. For all
1\leq i,j\leq 4
there exist rational numbers
\lambda_{i,j,k}
,
1\leq k\leq 4
such that

\nu_{i}\nu_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{4}\lambda_{ijk}\nu_{k}.

For all
{\mathbf{d}},{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{4}({\mathbf{d}}\diamond{\mathbf{e}})_{i}\nu_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{4}\Big(\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}\lambda_{ijk}d_{i}e_{j}\Big)\nu_{k}

Identifying the fourth coordinate, we deduce for all
{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
,

\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}=\Big\{{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}:\sum_{j=1}^{4}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{ij4}d_{i}\Big)e_{j}=0\Big\}.

The terms
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{ij4}d_{i}
, for
j=1,2,3,4
correspond to the coefficients of the fourth row of the matrix
in basis
{\mathbf{v}}

of the multiplication by
d=d_{1}\nu_{1}+d_{2}\nu_{2}+d_{3}\nu_{3}+d_{4}\nu_{4}
.
Since
{\mathbf{d}}\not={\mathbf{0}}
, this matrix is invertible and at least one of these coefficients is non zero.
This shows that
\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}
has rank
3
.
By
[
7
]
, the determinant of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{\,d\,}}
is equal to the determinant of the dual lattice that is for us the lattice spanned by the vector

T({\mathbf{d}}):=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i14}d_{i}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i24}d_{i}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i34}d_{i}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\lambda_{i44}d_{i}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(9.5)

Since the components of this vector have size
O(\max_{1\leq i\leq 4}|d_{i}|)
,
{\mathrm{\,det\,}}(\Lambda_{d})\ll\|{\mathbf{d}}\|
.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\geq 3
, we have

\sum_{\max(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|,|x_{3}|)\ll X}\tau\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\nu_{i}\Big)^{m}\ll X^{3}(\log X)^{O_{m}(1)}.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
[
14
, Lemma 4.2]
which concerns the case
\nu_{i}=\theta^{i-1}
.
The only place where this change could have an importance is for the bound of the sums with any
{\mathfrak{d}}
such that
N({\mathfrak{d}})\ll X^{1/n}

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\max(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|,|x_{3}|)\ll X}\\
{{\mathfrak{d}}|(\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\nu_{i})}\end{subarray}}1.

Since the
\nu_{i}
are linear combinations of some
\theta^{j}
,
j=0,1,2,3
for
\theta
such that
K=\mathbb{Q}(\theta)
,
the condition
{\mathfrak{d}}|(\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\nu_{i})
can be split in the
x_{i}
into arithmetic progression
\ (\mathrm{mod}\ N({\mathfrak{d}}))
,
and thus the argument of
[
14
]
combined with Lemma
9.4
apply also in our case.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
{\mathbf{d}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\cap[-D,D]^{4}
and

\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}
as in Lemma
9.7
. Let
{\mathbf{z}}_{1}({\mathbf{d}})
denote a shortest non-zero vector in
\Lambda_{\mathbf{d}}
. Then we have
\|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}({\mathbf{d}})\|\ll D^{1/3}
and

|\{{\mathbf{d}}\in[1,D]^{n}:\|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}({\mathbf{d}})\|\leq Z\}|\ll D^{3+o(1)}Z^{3}.

Furthermore we have

\sum_{\|{\mathbf{d}}\|\leq D}\frac{1}{\|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}({\mathbf{d}})\|^{2}}\ll D^{10/3+o(1)}.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the proof of
[
14
, Lemma 7.3]
except that we have a slightly different definition for
\diamond
, and so require Lemmas
9.7
and
9.8
instead of
[
14
, Lemma 4.2]
and
[
14
, Lemma 7.2]
.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be an ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
with
(N({\mathfrak{d}}),q)=1
. Let
{\mathcal{R}}\subset[-X,X]^{3}
as in the Proposition
9.11
below.
Then we have

|\big\{{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\cap{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathfrak{d}}|\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i}\big),\ {\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{a}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\}|=\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{d}}){\mathrm{\,vol\,}}({\mathcal{R}})}{N({\mathfrak{d}})q^{3}}+O(N({\mathfrak{d}})^{4}X^{2}).

Proof.

The proof is identical as the proof of
[
14
, Lemma 7.4]
with
{\mathbf{v}}
in place of
(1,\ldots,\theta^{n-1})
. In fact, the arguments of
[
14
]
give a slightly stronger error term of
O(X^{2}\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}(N(\delta)(qN(\delta))^{-2}+\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}(N(\delta)))
.
∎

9.3
Sums of Type I

We now state a similar result to
[
14
, Proposition 7.5]

Proposition 9.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}\subset[-X,X]^{3}
be a region such that any line parallel to the coordinate axes intersects
{\mathcal{R}}
in
O(1)
intervals.
For any given
{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
and
q\leq\sqrt{X}
we define

\Gamma=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i}:{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\cap{\mathcal{R}},\ {\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\Big\}.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{d}}=\{\kappa\in\Gamma:{\mathfrak{d}}|(\kappa)\}
.
Then we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{N({\mathfrak{d}})\in[D,2D]}\\
{(N({\mathfrak{d}}),q)=1}\end{subarray}}\Bigg||\Gamma_{\mathfrak{d}}|-\frac{\varrho_{\mathbf{v}}({\mathfrak{d}}){\mathrm{\,vol\,}}({\mathcal{R}})}{q^{3}N({\mathfrak{d}})}\Bigg|\ll X^{2}q^{1+o(1)}D^{1/3+o(1)}+Dq^{4+o(1)}.

(9.6)

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
14
, Proposition 7.5]
, but now we work with a general order
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{v}}
in place of
\mathbb{Z}[\theta]
. This involves minor modifications at the beginning of the argument; the last steps require no modification. For brevity we emphasise just the key points requiring modification and only sketch the rest of th argument.

We split the summation on the ideals
{\mathfrak{d}}
according to their class in
Cl_{K}
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a given class and consider the contribution of all the
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Since the
{\mathfrak{d}}
in the summation in (
9.6
) are coprime with
q
,
we can fix a representative integral ideal
{\mathfrak{c}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
(N({\mathfrak{c}}),q)=1
and with
N({\mathfrak{c}})=q^{o(1)}
.
The ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}(N({\mathfrak{c}}))
is a principal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
. By Lemma
9.2
we can find a generator
of the form
\delta=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}d_{i}\nu_{i}
where the
d_{i}
are integers such that

|d_{i}|\ll D^{1/n}q^{o(1)}
. Then
\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}:=\frac{1}{WN({\mathfrak{c}})}\sum_{i=1}^{4}d_{i}\nu_{i}
is a generator of the principal fractional ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}
. In
[
14
]
it is proved that
|\sigma_{0}(\delta_{\mathfrak{c}})|\gg D^{1/4}q^{o(1)}
for all embeddings
\sigma_{0}
.

Let
\alpha\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{d}}
, so
(\alpha)={\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{d}}
for some integral ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}
. Since
(\alpha)={\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}
and
(\alpha)
and
{\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}=(\delta_{\mathfrak{c}})
are principal,
{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}
is principal too, so

{\mathfrak{a}}^{\prime}{\mathfrak{c}}=(\beta)
for some generator
\beta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
By Lemma
9.1
, we can take
\beta=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}b_{i}\nu_{i}
where
{\mathbf{b}}=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
satisfies

({\mathbf{b}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ W))\in{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
. Then
(\alpha)=(\beta)(\delta_{\mathfrak{c}})
.
Let
{\mathbf{d}}=(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4})
. We have
W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})\beta\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}=\sum_{k=1}^{4}({\mathbf{d}}\diamond{\mathbf{b}})_{k}\nu_{k}
.

\beta\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}=\sum_{k=1}^{4}\frac{1}{W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})}\Big(\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}\ell_{i,j,k}b_{i}d_{i}\Big)\nu_{k}.

The coefficient of
\nu_{i}
are integers if and only
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}
satisfy some congruences modulo
W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})
.
We also need to impose that
{\mathfrak{c}}|(\beta)
. This is also equivalent to some congruences conditions modulo
W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})
for
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}
.
Let
q_{1}=[q,W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})]
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}^{\prime}\subset\{0\ldots,q_{1}-1\}^{4}
the set of r classes satisfying all these conditions and furthermore such that

({\mathrm{\,d\,}}\diamond{\mathbf{b}})_{4}\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1})\ \text{and}\ \frac{({\mathrm{\,d\,}}\diamond{\mathbf{b}})_{i}}{W^{2}N({\mathfrak{c}})}\equiv{({\mathbf{u}}_{0})_{i}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\ \text{for}\ 1\leq i\leq 3.

Thus, for
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we are interested in

|\Gamma_{\mathfrak{d}}|=\sum_{{\mathbf{b}}_{0}\in V_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{\mathbf{b}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\\
{{\mathbf{b}}\equiv{\mathbf{b}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1})}\\
{\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}\beta\in\Gamma}\end{subarray}}1.

The rest of the proof follows
[
14
]
. Let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{\,d\,}}
be the lattice introduced in Lemma
9.7
.
We write
{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{b}}^{(1)}+q_{1}{\mathbf{b}}^{(2)}
where
{\mathbf{b}}^{(1)}
is some vector of
\Lambda_{{\mathrm{\,d\,}}}
such that

{\mathbf{b}}^{(1)}\equiv{\mathbf{b}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1})
(when such
{\mathbf{b}}^{(1)}
exists) and
{\mathbf{b}}^{(2)}\in\Lambda_{{\mathfrak{d}}_{\mathfrak{c}}}

|\Gamma_{\mathfrak{d}}|=\sideset{}{{}^{\prime\prime}}{\sum}_{{\mathbf{b}}_{0}\in V_{0}^{\prime}}{}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{\mathbf{b}}^{(2)}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{\,d\,}}}\\
{{\mathbf{b}}\equiv{\mathbf{b}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q_{1})}\\
{\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}\beta_{1}+q_{1}\delta_{\mathfrak{c}}\beta_{2}\in\Gamma}\end{subarray}}1,

where
\sum^{\prime\prime}
indicates that the
{\mathbf{b}}_{0}
are as above but furthermore such that there exists a vector
b^{(1)}
in the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{d}}
and
\beta_{j}=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=1}^{4}b_{i}^{(j)}\nu_{i}
for
j=1,2
. The argument now follows the proof of
[
14
, Proposition 7.5]
precisely, except that we apply Lemmas
9.8
,
9.10
for the basis
{\mathbf{v}}
in place of
[
14
, Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4]
.
∎

9.4
Initial steps in the Type II sum

If
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{q_{1}\cdots q_{\ell}}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},m,p)
then there exists
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N(a_{1}\nu_{1}+a_{2}\nu_{2}+a_{3}\nu_{3})=d\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}q_{i}
.
The conditions on
q_{i}
imply that
(m,q_{1}\cdots q_{\ell})=1
but in general, it is not clear that
(d,m)=1
.
This may gives some complications in the application of Proposition
9.11
.
Let us write
m_{0}=(d,m^{\infty})
and recall the notation

{\mathcal{X}}=\prod_{i=1}^{3}[X_{i},X_{i}(1+\eta_{1})[
from (
4.1
). In almost cases,
m_{0}
is small. The contribution of the
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, such that
{\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m)
and
m_{0}>D_{0}

with
D_{0}=\eta_{\mathcal{X}}^{-2}\eta_{1}^{-1}
with
\eta_{\mathcal{X}}
defined by (
9.11
) below, is less than

\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m_{0}|m^{\infty}}\\
{m_{0}>D_{0}}\end{subarray}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{X}}}\\
{{\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m)}\\
{N\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i}\big)\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m_{0})}\end{subarray}}1\ll\eta_{1}^{3}\prod_{i=1}^{3}X_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m_{0}|m^{\infty}}\\
{m_{0}>D_{0}}\end{subarray}}\frac{4^{\omega(m_{0})}}{m_{0}m^{2}}\\
&\qquad\ll\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}\prod_{i=1}^{3}X_{i}}{m^{2}\sqrt{D_{0}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m_{0}|m^{\infty}}\\
{m_{0}>D_{0}}\end{subarray}}\frac{4^{\omega(m_{0})}}{\sqrt{m_{0}}}\ll\frac{\eta_{1}^{3}\prod_{i=1}^{3}X_{i}c^{\Omega(m)}}{m^{2}\sqrt{D_{0}}},\end{split}

for some
c>0
large enough.
This contribution is sufficiently small.

We now suppose that
m_{0}\leq D_{0}
.

Let

\begin{split}{\mathcal{M}}(m_{0})&=\Big\{{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\in[1,mm_{0}]^{3}:{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\equiv{u_{0}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m),\\
&\ N(\sum_{i=1}^{3}({\mathbf{v}}_{0})_{i}\nu_{i})\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m_{0}),\ \big(m,\frac{N(\sum_{i=1}^{3}({\mathbf{v}}_{0})_{i}\nu_{i}}{m_{0}}\big)=1\Big\}.\end{split}

(9.7)

Then for every
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{A}}({\mathbf{u}}_{0},m)
such that
m_{0}=(N(a_{1}\nu_{1}+a_{2}\nu_{2}+a_{3}\nu_{3}),m^{\infty})
,
there exists exactly one
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m_{0})
such that
{\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ mm_{0})
.

We deduce that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{X}}}\\
{{\mathbf{a}}\equiv{\mathbf{u}}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ m)}\\
{(N(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i}),m^{\infty})\leq D_{0}}\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m_{0}|m^{\infty}}\\
{m_{0}\leq D_{0}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{{\mathbf{v}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m_{0})}|{\mathcal{A}}({\mathbf{v}}_{0},mm_{0})|.

(9.8)

Any
{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{A}}({\mathbf{v}}_{0},mm_{0})
is such that the associated ideal
(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i})
may be factored as

(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i})={\mathfrak{M}}_{0}{\mathfrak{J}}
with
N({\mathfrak{M}}_{0})=m_{0}
and
(N({\mathfrak{J}}),m)=1
. This property will simplify some GCD considerations in the next sections. Let

m^{\prime}:=m_{0}m

(9.9)

denote this extended modulus.

9.5
Switching to ideals with norms in small boxes

We introduce the sets of principal ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}=\Big\{\big(\sum_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}\nu_{i}\big):{\mathbf{a}}\in{\mathcal{X}}\Big\}.

(9.10)

For any
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}
there is exactly one
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in{\mathcal{X}}
such that
